The text does not say what part of him this comes from, how strongly he feels it, or what it leads to next; it does not even say where inside him it lives, only that it is the whole man. What stands out, plainly, is a freedom to give that the tight-fisted never have — and that freedom is the mark of a good inner life.

### H2: Psa 45:1-15

#### Story
In this royal wedding poem, grace is not something the king does but something he carries: a charm and winning favour that rests on his very words — grace is 'poured upon his lips.' It is a quality he has, a bearing, not an act reaching out to anyone. Notice what the poem does not say: this grace goes out to no one and accomplishes nothing here; it simply adorns him, and God has blessed him for it. It sits in no single part of him, has no measured strength, and leads to nothing further that is named. What we are left with is grace as pure attractiveness — favour and charm resting on a man — before it is ever a kindness shown to someone else.

### H2: Psa 55:1-5

#### Story
In this reading mercy is not something given but something desperately asked for. The psalmist, surrounded by dread and inner anguish, cries to God: do not hide from my plea. This plea is the very heart of his prayer — a humbled cry that claims nothing as owed and asks only for undeserved kindness. It is aimed entirely at God, reaching out beyond himself. The text does not say where in him it starts, how loud it is, or what it achieves; it simply shows a person in trouble turning upward, empty-handed, and begging for grace he cannot demand. Where grace can be a charm a man possesses, here it is a favour he lacks and must ask for.

### H2: Psa 72:13-15

#### Story
In this song about the ideal king, his greatness is measured not by his power but by his tenderness: he has pity on the weak and the needy. This pity is not a fleeting feeling; it is bound up with actually rescuing them and saving their lives — the mercy inside him is what drives the saving he does. It is a settled part of who he is as king. The text does not say where the feeling comes from, how intense it is, or track any separate result; it simply shows that real royal greatness bends toward the lowest and most helpless. The inside of good power, here, is compassion aimed at those who have no strength to claim it.

### H2: Psa 102:12-16

#### Story
In this reading, pity is aimed at a ruined city. God's servants feel tenderness even for Zion's dust — they hold her broken stones dear and grieve over the rubble. It is a genuine leaning of the heart, not just an action, and it is knit together with that love for her stones. The text does not say where the feeling begins, how strong it is, or what it produces; it simply shows hearts moved by ruin. What is striking is that this compassion is stirred not by a suffering person but by a broken place — the caring heart can ache over wreckage and rubble, loving what is shattered simply because it matters to them.

### H2: Psa 103:8-14

#### Story
This reading looks at the human picture used to explain God's mercy: a father's tenderness toward his children. The point of the verse is God's compassion, but what is actually captured here is the father's — the deep, settled softness of a parent's heart for his own, set right alongside the way the Lord cares for those who revere him. The text does not say where this fatherly feeling comes from, how strong it runs, or what it leads to; it simply holds it up. And that is the striking thing: the most tender thing we know in ordinary life, a parent bending over a child, is taken as the yardstick that makes God's mercy understandable.

### H2: Psa 109:1-8

#### Story
In this bitter psalm of cursing, pity shows up only so that it can be refused. The psalmist wishes that no one — not a single person — would ever show kindness to his enemy's orphaned children. The tender feeling is named on purpose, precisely to be shut off, tied to the kindness he also wants withheld. This is the harsh side of the human heart shown without excuse: not softness here but a wish for payback, that a merciless man should himself find no mercy for his own. The text gives no reason, no measure, no outcome — just the bare will that pity be denied. It shows that even the word for tenderness can be swung like a weapon.

### H2: Psa 109:14-22

#### Story
A little later the psalm names exactly what made the enemy guilty: he never remembered to be kind. Here the failure is his own — not something wished on him, but the real fault in him. Instead of showing mercy he hunted down the poor, the needy, and the broken-hearted to destroy them. His crime is traced back to something inside: kindness simply never crossed his mind. No cause is given, no measure, no separate outcome — just this settled blank where mercy should have been. The lesson is unsettling: a person can be condemned not only for cruel acts but for an inner life in which kindness was never even remembered.

### H2: Psa 112:1-10

#### Story
In this picture of a person who reveres God, grace turns into a human character trait: he is gracious, merciful, and upright, all together. Grace here is a settled kindly leaning toward other people, one thread woven in with mercy and honesty into a whole good character. And the quiet point is that the very quality the psalms elsewhere say God has, this ordinary faithful person now carries — he mirrors it toward the people around him. The text does not say where it comes from in him, how strong it is, or what it produces; it simply names it. What is striking across the family is the shift: grace that was once a king's charm, or a favour begged from God, is here plain neighbourly kindness in an everyday good person.

### H2: Psa 116:1-10

#### Story
This reading catches the cry for mercy after it has been answered. The psalmist begins, 'I love the Lord, because he has heard my voice and my pleas' — so the plea is remembered as one that was already granted. It is something he did, reaching out entirely toward God. And the response inside him is love: because God bent down to listen, affection wells up. Unlike a raw cry of distress, this looks back with gratitude. The text does not say where the plea began in him, how loud it was, or track a separate result; it simply shows that asking for mercy and receiving it opened a door — and through that door love for God came in.

### H2: Psa 130:1-8

#### Story
Here the cry for mercy is still going up, unanswered, from the very bottom: 'out of the depths I cry to you — let your ears hear my pleas.' It is something the psalmist is doing, reaching out toward God from as low as a person can be. And the plea rests on a stark admission that comes next in the psalm: if God kept score of sins, no one could survive it — but there is forgiveness with him. So this is the cry of someone who knows he has no claim of his own and throws himself entirely on God's mercy. The text names no source, no measure, no result — just a soul at its lowest, calling upward, with nothing to stand on but grace.

### H2: Psa 140:1-9

#### Story
In this psalm the enemies work in silence — plotting evil in their hearts, sharpening their tongues like snakes, hiding traps along the path. Against all that quiet menace, the psalmist has one response: he raises his voice and pleads for mercy. Here the cry is felt as much as done — an emotional surge sounded out loud — and it is tied to the very voice that carries it, an appeal thrown upward. He does not answer scheming with scheming; he answers hidden threats with an open, pleading cry. The text names no cause, measure, or outcome for it. What it shows is a person who, when surrounded and endangered, meets the silence of his enemies by crying out for mercy.

### H2: Psa 141:4-10

#### Story
The last and most surprising kindness in this group is a blow taken as love. The psalmist says: let a good man strike me — that is a kindness; let him correct me — it is like soothing oil on my head; I will not refuse it. Here kindness is not something he gives but something he receives, and he finds it in what feels like an attack. His inner life is so well-ordered that he welcomes an honest rebuke as care rather than injury, and even embraces the sting. Surrounded by tempting, corrupt company whose delicacies he turns down, he would rather take a good man's hard words than a bad man's treats. The text gives no cause or measure — just a heart that recognizes love in correction and gladly takes the wound.


---

## File: psalms__grief-lament-sorrow__narratives.md

# grief-lament-sorrow — Psalms narratives

### H2: Psa 5:1-12

#### Story
The psalm begins not with polished words but with a groan. Before he can shape a single request, the man is simply making a wordless sound of distress, and his first act is to ask God to pay attention to that sound rather than to any argument. The groan rises straight out of him - nothing outside is blamed for it, and it is not felt in any one part of the body but is the whole self aching. He does not throw it out at random; he turns it deliberately toward God and asks to be understood beneath his words. Nothing here rates how loud or how strong it is, and no result is recorded - the point is only that the deepest part of a person can speak to God in a groan before it can speak in sentences.

### H2: Psa 6:1-10

#### Story
Every night the man cries until his bed is soaked and his couch is drenched. This is grief that has become physical - tears pouring out in the dark hours, night after night, until the bedding itself is wet through. Nothing outside is named as the cause; the weeping simply overflows from the whole of him, not from any single part. The account is careful to mark this off from mere tiredness: this is the active flood of sorrow, the visible measure of how much he is hurting. It does not put a number on the tears, but the drenched bed says enough - a person coming apart quietly, alone, every night.

### H2: Psa 12:1-4

#### Story
Here the groan belongs not to the singer but to the poor and the plundered - the people who have been robbed and have nothing left but the sound of their suffering. Their groan is not weak: it reaches God and moves him to stand up and act. It is a wordless cry, rising from the whole of crushed people, and it is exactly this sound that God answers. Nothing measures how loud it is, and the account keeps the rescue tied to the cry itself rather than treating it as a separate outcome. The lesson is stark and hopeful at once: the bare groan of the helpless is enough to make God rise.

### H2: Psa 30:3-12

#### Story
Here weeping is not happening - it is being understood. The man has stepped back far enough to see the shape of his own sorrow: God's anger lasts only a moment, but his kindness lasts a lifetime, so tears may stay through the night, but joy arrives with the morning. This is grief turned into a hard-won piece of wisdom, a settled conviction that sorrow is temporary and gladness is the sure dawn. Nothing outside is blamed and no strength of feeling is measured; it is simply the whole self learning to trust the shape of its own pain. Weeping, seen this way, is bound to the morning that ends it.

### H2: Psa 32:1-11

#### Story
This is the grief of a guilty conscience that will not let go. Day and night the man feels God's hand pressing down on him like a weight, and his strength drains away as if he were left out in the blazing heat of summer. It is not sorrow over a loss but the crushing pressure of sin he has not yet admitted - the whole of him wrung dry, not just some part. The account does not rate how bad it is, but 'day and night' and 'dried up' say plenty. He is pressed down until the only relief left is to confess, and God's heavy hand is what finally squeezes the truth out of him.

### H2: Psa 35:9-15

#### Story
When his enemies were sick, the man grieved for them as if they were his own family - walking about in sorrow as though mourning a friend, a brother, even his own mother. This is real, deep grief, the kind reserved for the closest of kin, and he spent it on the very people who hated him. Nothing measures how strong it was; it simply came from the whole of him. The bitter twist, which the text does not hide, is that when he himself later stumbled, those same people gathered and rejoiced. He gave them a family's tears and got mockery in return.

### H2: Psa 38:1-20

#### Story
Worn down and crushed, the man groans - and the groan is pushed out by the churning inside him. His heart is in uproar, and that turmoil cannot stay quiet; it breaks out of him as a raw, involuntary sound. The groan comes from the whole of him, not from any single place, even though it is the heart's commotion driving it. Nothing rates how loud or how bad it is. It is simply the noise a person makes when the storm inside has nowhere else to go - inner turmoil turned audible.

### H2: Psa 42:1-11

#### Story
For this man, tears have become his food, day and night. He is not crying about something in particular or crying to get anything - the weeping has stopped being an action and become his whole condition, as steady as meals. He lives on his tears the way another lives on bread. All the while, people around him keep jeering, 'Where is your God?' - and that taunt keeps the tears coming. There is no measure of it and no outcome named; there is only a person so soaked in grief that sorrow itself has become what feeds him.

### H2: Psa 43:1-5

#### Story
Again a man walks about darkened with grief, exactly as in the psalm before - mourning as a whole way of moving, not sorrow over one thing. And again two forces press him down: the weight of enemies who oppress him, and the feeling that God has turned away. But the wound has sharpened. Before, it was the sense of being forgotten; now it is the sense of being cast off, actively rejected by the God he asks to defend him. The grief looks the same, but it comes from a colder place. He goes about in shadow because he feels not merely overlooked but pushed away.

### H2: Psa 55:1-5

#### Story
Underneath his words there is a sound with no words in it. As the man pours out his complaint, he is also moaning - a low, restless noise of a heart in turmoil that spills past anything he can actually say. It is not aimed at anyone and has no particular shape; it is just raw distress. It comes with a body that cannot keep still, so the whole of him is stirred up at once. This is the part of grief that runs deeper than speech - the moan that escapes when words are not enough.

### H2: Psa 55:9-23

#### Story
Three times a day - evening, morning, and noon - the man pours out his complaint to God, and folded into that prayer is a moan. It is a wordless groan, the sound of a heart in turmoil, and it is not thrown out at random: it is kept up steadily as part of his regular crying out to God. He has no polished words for it and no particular style; it is simply the turmoil inside, offered again and again. What makes this different is the discipline of it - grief brought back to God on a schedule, in confidence that God hears.

### H2: Psa 56:1-13

#### Story
The man asks God to catch his tears in a bottle and to write them down in his book - and he believes God actually does. His weeping is not wasted or ignored; every tear is gathered up and counted as precious, kept safe by God himself. The tears come from his sleepless, restless nights of tossing and turning, but the striking thing is not how he sheds them - it is how they are stored. This is the tenderest picture of care in the whole psalm: grief that no one else sees, treasured by God as if each drop mattered.

### H2: Psa 64:1-6

#### Story
This bitterness does not belong to the sufferer - it belongs to wicked men, and it is a weapon. They sharpen their tongues like swords and load their bitter words like arrows, taking aim from hiding at people who have done nothing wrong. The venom sits in the tongue itself, the tool they use to wound, and it works hand in hand with their secret plots. This is not grief that hurts inside; it is malice pointed outward, cruelty shaped into speech and fired from ambush. Read plainly, it shows the inside of cruel people: their bitterness is a loaded, aimed thing, targeted at the innocent.

### H2: Psa 69:9-10

#### Story
When the man wept and went without food to humble himself before God, it was true devotion - grief poured out in worship, his very soul brought low as an offering. But his piety backfired in the eyes of others: the scoffers took his weeping and fasting and turned it into a joke, a byword to sneer at. The tears were aimed at God, sincerely; the mockery came from people who twisted his sincerity against him. This is the pain of having your deepest act of humility used to shame you - grief offered up to God, thrown back as an insult by the crowd.

### H2: Psa 77:1-3

#### Story
In the dark, the man remembers God - and instead of comforting him, it makes him moan. Thinking about God, which should steady a troubled heart, only loosens a low groan of distress. The moan has no particular target; it is simply the sound of a mind dwelling on God and finding no relief. The text does not soften this: remembering God here brings turmoil, not peace. It is an honest picture of a faith in crisis, where the very thought of God deepens the ache rather than easing it.

### H2: Psa 77:5-18

#### Story
Here grief becomes a turning point. Instead of only feeling his pain, the man names it out loud - 'this is my grief: that God's strong hand seems to have changed toward me.' And in the very act of naming the wound, something shifts. Saying plainly what hurts becomes the doorway out: from here he turns from brooding on God's silence to deliberately calling to mind the things God has done. This is grief owned and spoken rather than merely endured, and the speaking is what lets hope back in. Putting the pain into words is the hinge the whole psalm swings on.

### H2: Psa 78:37-46

#### Story
This grief is not the singer's - it is the grief the people caused God. Again and again in the wilderness they rebelled, and their rebellion actually wounded God, causing him pain. The direction is reversed from the usual picture: instead of a person hurting and crying to God, here people are the ones inflicting the hurt, and God is the one grieved. Nothing qualifies how or how much; it is simply stated that their defiance pained him. It is a sobering thought - that stubborn rebellion is not just disobedience but something that genuinely grieves the God against whom it is aimed.

### H2: Psa 79:11-13

#### Story
The prayer here asks God to let the groans of the prisoners rise up before him. These are captives - a suffering people locked away - and their groaning is treated as a prayer in its own right, lifted deliberately into God's presence. It goes hand in hand with a plea that God would spare those under sentence of death. The groan is not measured or described in any special way; it is simply the raw sound of imprisoned suffering, set before God so that he will hear it and act. Their sighing becomes their petition.

### H2: Psa 80:4-7

#### Story
This is a whole nation weeping, not just one person. Their tears have become their food and their drink - God has fed them with the bread of tears and made them drink tears by the bowlful. Grief has become so constant that it is what they live on, morning and night. And the text names God himself as the one who has served them this diet of sorrow. It is corporate anguish, a people so soaked in weeping that tears are their bread and their water - and the God they cry to is the one who has given it to them.

### H2: Psa 88:7-12

#### Story
Day after day this man simply suffers. His sorrow never lifts - it is constant, wearing him down until even his eyes grow weak and dim from the weight of it. Unlike most of these psalms, no relief comes and no bright turn arrives; the grief just goes on. It is the kind of ongoing affliction that quietly ruins the body over time, and it fills the whole of him. He still cries out to God, but here, in the bleakest of these songs, the sorrow is left standing with no answer - an honest picture of grief that does not resolve.

### H2: Psa 102:1-5

#### Story
The man's groaning has become so loud and so constant that it is wasting his body away - he is so worn down that his bones seem to cling to his skin. This is grief that never stops long enough to let him recover; it groans on and on in his misery until his frame is thin and consumed. The groan is bound up with his whole inner state and shows itself in his shrunken body. There is no measure put on it and no relief noted here - just a suffering man whose endless groaning is literally eating him away.

### H2: Psa 102:8-10

#### Story
This man's food is ashes and his drink is mixed with his own tears. He weeps so much that his crying spills into his cup - when he drinks, he is swallowing his own tears along with the water. Grief has soaked into the most basic acts of eating and drinking, so that mourning is not only something he does but something he takes in. Nothing measures how much, and no relief is noted here. It is a picture of a person so steeped in sorrow that even his meals taste of it.

### H2: Psa 102:18-23

#### Story
God looks down from heaven and hears the groaning of prisoners - the sighing of people locked away and under sentence of death. Their groan is not wasted: it reaches God, who bends down to listen, and it is bound up with their being set free. The sound is simply the raw distress of captives, but it is heard, and hearing it God moves to release those condemned to die. Here the groan is not left hanging; it is answered from above, and freedom follows.

### H2: Psa 106:22-32

#### Story
The people wore Moses down until they made his spirit bitter. This is bitterness done to a person - the constant provoking of the crowd souring the inside of a good man until something curdled in him. And that embittered spirit had a consequence: it spilled out in rash, unguarded words. The chain is simple and human: they goaded him, his spirit turned bitter, and then he spoke without thinking. Even a leader like Moses could be pushed to the point where the bitterness others stirred up in him broke out of his mouth.

### H2: Psa 107:37-43

#### Story
This is the sorrow of a whole people who have been beaten down. Through oppression and cruelty and trouble, they are made few and brought low, and grief is what fills them in that humbled state. It is not one person's private ache but a community's suffering, tied to their losses and their crushing. Nothing here measures it or explains it away. It stands as the low point of their story - a people diminished and grieving - within a larger song that goes on to tell how such fortunes can be turned around.

### H2: Psa 116:1-10

#### Story
Here the man looks back on tears that God has already dried. He remembers weeping, but he tells of it as something now over: God rescued his soul from death, kept his feet from stumbling, and delivered his eyes from tears. The grief is real, but it is spoken from the safe side of it - the crying has been wiped away by God's rescue. This is not sorrow in the middle of the storm but sorrow remembered with relief, the weeping stopped and the tears wiped clean because deliverance came.

### H2: Psa 126:5-6

#### Story
Here tears are like seed being planted. Those who go out weeping as they scatter their seed will come back singing when the harvest comes in. The grief is real - hard, sorrowful labour - but it is not wasted; it is an investment, and what it yields is joy. This is weeping with a promise attached: the sorrow of today's work becomes the seed of tomorrow's gladness. It is grief pointed forward to a harvest, the tears of sowing turning into shouts of joy at the reaping.

### H2: Psa 137:6-7

#### Story
By the rivers of Babylon, far from home, the exiles sat down and wept - and what set them weeping was remembering. The moment they thought of Zion, their lost city, the tears came. This is a whole community grieving together, not one person's private sorrow, and its trigger is memory: the sight of a foreign land bringing back everything they had lost. There is no measuring it - just displaced people whose remembering breaks into weeping. It is the ache of homesickness for a place taken from them.

### H2: Psa 139:21-24

#### Story
This 'grievous' is not the man's own pain but something wrong inside him that he asks God to find. He opens himself completely and invites God to search him and see whether there is any hurtful or false way in him - and if there is, to lead him out of it onto the everlasting way. It is an act of honest self-examination: rather than hiding his faults, he wants them exposed so they can be dealt with. He hands the inside of himself over to God's inspection, willing to have whatever is harmful uncovered and removed. It is grief's word turned into a request to be corrected.


---

## File: psalms__hope-waiting__narratives.md

# hope-waiting — Psalms narratives

### H2: Psa 5:1-12

#### Story
At first light the psalmist deliberately sets his prayer before God, laying it out as carefully as an offering, and then stays awake and alert for a reply. This is a chosen discipline, not a mood that washes over him: he readies himself and then watches, and the two acts are really one steady posture of expectation. The psalm does not say what first moved him to it, nor does it locate the waiting in any one part of him - it is his whole self leaning forward. It also does not tell us how intensely he waits or what finally comes of it; the watching is simply left open, its answer still ahead. What we see is a person schooling himself each morning to wait on God.

### H2: Psa 9:1-19

#### Story
The psalm makes a quiet but stubborn claim: the hope carried by the poor and forgotten will not be crushed out of them. However long they are overlooked, that inner expectation survives. The psalm does not explain where such hope comes from or pin it to any single feeling; it simply insists the hope lasts. It does not measure how strong it is or spell out how it is finally rewarded within the verse - only that it will not perish. The focus stays on the durable inner life of people the world ignores.

### H2: Psa 23:1-6

#### Story
The psalm ends by settling: the writer knows he will live in God's house forever. This is not a plan he is working toward but a deep, restful certainty of belonging that has no end. The psalm does not argue for it or trace where it came from - it simply lands there, as the place the whole song has been heading. It gives no measure of the feeling and names no further result, because the permanence is the point. What we are left with is a person completely at home, and unbrokenly so, near God.

### H2: Psa 25:1-3

#### Story
The psalm draws a line between two kinds of people: those who wait on God, and those who betray without cause. The ones who wait, it promises, will never be humiliated for it. This is trust stretched out into patience - a settled inner leaning on God that is bound to be honoured rather than let down. The psalm does not say what first produces such waiting or how strong it must be; it simply guarantees the waiter will not be put to shame. The whole weight rests on the quiet confidence of the one who waits.

### H2: Psa 25:5-22

#### Story
Now the waiting becomes personal and unbroken: the psalmist tells God he waits for him all day long, and in the same breath asks to be taught and led in the truth. This is not a passing glance upward but a whole day spent leaning on God. The psalm does not say what sparked it or how fiercely it burns; it simply stretches the waiting across every hour. The picture is of a person whose entire day is coloured by expectation toward God.

### H2: Psa 27:11-14

#### Story
This time waiting takes the form of an order the psalmist gives himself: wait for the LORD, be strong, let your heart be brave, and wait again. Waiting here is anything but limp - it takes courage, and he has to rally himself to it. The psalm does not say what prompted the self-talk or how hard the struggle is; it simply shows a person bracing his own inner life to keep waiting. It is the sound of someone stiffening his resolve to stay hopeful when it would be easier to give up.

### H2: Psa 33:15-22

#### Story
Those who set their hope on God's faithful love are the very people God keeps his eye on. Hope here is a leaning-forward of the heart, resting its whole weight on God's steady, covenant love. The psalm does not explain how the hope began or measure its strength; it simply says this kind of hope is what draws God's attention. Where fear looks up in awe, this hope leans forward in expectation - and it is met by God's watching care.

### H2: Psa 37:1-12

#### Story
The instruction is to go quiet: be still before God and wait for him patiently, and stop working yourself up over people who seem to get away with everything. Waiting here means calming down - deliberately refusing to let the success of the wicked stir you into anxiety. The psalm does not say where the calm comes from or how hard it is to hold; it simply commands the inner life to settle. It is the picture of a restless heart being told to be quiet and trust.

### H2: Psa 37:14-40

#### Story
Wait for the LORD and keep to his path - do both, and he will lift you up to inherit the land. Waiting here is not sitting idle; it walks God's way while it waits. The two go together: expectation and obedience are one posture. The psalm does not spell out where such waiting comes from or how intense it must be, and it folds the promised reward into the command itself. The picture is of someone who keeps living rightly precisely because he is still waiting on God to act.

### H2: Psa 38:1-20

#### Story
Surrounded by accusers and having chosen to stay silent, the psalmist turns his whole hope toward God: it is you I wait for, and you who will answer. Waiting here is the moment he stops defending himself and hands the response over to God. The psalm does not say how the hope began or how strong it is; it simply shows him leaning everything on God being the one to speak. It is the quiet pivot of a cornered man from self-defence to trust.

### H2: Psa 39:1-13

#### Story
Having just called all of life fleeting and empty, the psalmist asks himself what he is even waiting for - and answers: my hope is in you. Every other thing he might have waited on has fallen away as vapour, and what remains is God alone. The psalm does not explain how the hope arose or measure it; it simply shows every lesser expectation collapsing into one. It is the sound of a disillusioned man discovering that God is the only thing left worth hoping in.

### H2: Psa 40:1-17

#### Story
Looking back, the psalmist testifies: I waited and waited for the LORD, and he leaned down and heard my cry. Here the waiting is not left hanging - it is remembered as answered, patience that God actually bent down to meet. The psalm does not say how the waiting began or how long it stretched, only that it was quiet and sustained and that God responded. It is the grateful memory of a person whose patience was rewarded by being heard.

### H2: Psa 42:1-11

#### Story
For the third time in the psalm the writer turns and speaks to his own sinking soul: why are you cast down? Hope in God. This closing repetition is a discipline - each time despair rises, he answers it by commanding himself to hope, and he names what that hope rests on: God, his salvation. Hope here is a deliberate act the self orders, aimed squarely at God. It is a man refusing to let his own dejection have the last word, steering his whole inner life back toward the God he trusts to save him.

### H2: Psa 52:1-9

#### Story
The psalm closes by turning waiting into worship: I will wait for your name, the writer says, because it is good - and he says it out loud among God's faithful people. His hope is not pinned to how things happen to turn out but to the kind of God he knows God to be. Waiting and thanksgiving run together here, and it is done in public, among the godly, not in private. It is a person confidently looking to God's good character, and glad to say so where others can hear.

### H2: Psa 56:1-13

#### Story
The same word that everywhere else means hoping in God is here twisted into something sinister: the psalmist's enemies wait for his life, patiently watching his every move for the chance to strike. Their waiting is a kind of dark hope - not for God, but for his death. The psalm does not explain what drives them; it just shows the patient, expectant menace of it. It is a chilling reminder that the human capacity to wait and hope can be bent toward murder as easily as toward God.

### H2: Psa 59:1-5

#### Story
The psalm opens in alarm: look, they are lying in ambush for my life. These are violent men crouched and ready, waiting for the moment to seize him. It is a different word from the ones used for hoping in God, but it describes the same posture turned evil - patient watching aimed at destruction. The psalm gives no reason for the hatred; it simply sounds the alarm of a man who knows he is being hunted.

### H2: Psa 62:1-2

#### Story
For God alone my soul waits in silence - this is the still centre of the whole psalm. The waiting is not a restless watching but a deep quiet: the soul has stopped its own striving and simply rests on God, from whom rescue will come. And the silence is not empty or passive; it is the most active kind of trust, a stillness that is itself an act of faith. Of all the ways this psalm speaks of waiting, this is the fullest - it tells us where the quiet comes from, that it rises from deep within him, and that it is for God alone. It is a person wholly at rest in God.

### H2: Psa 62:4-12

#### Story
Now the psalmist turns and tells his own soul to do what the psalm's opening described: for God alone be still, because my hope comes from him. Here hope is the reason the silence is possible - the soul can stop striving precisely because its whole future rests in God's hands. It is a quiet, settled expectation, wearing the same stillness as before, and pointed entirely at God as its source. It is a man grounding his own calm in the fact that everything he hopes for depends on God alone.

### H2: Psa 65:1-6

#### Story
This psalm stretches hope to the horizon: God is the confidence of everyone to the ends of the earth and the farthest seas. It is no longer one person's trust but all of creation's, resting on the God who saves and answers with awesome, righteous deeds. The reach is deliberately universal - as far as land and sea go, so far does this hope extend. It is a vision of the whole world's inner life leaning, knowingly or not, on the one God who puts things right.

### H2: Psa 69:3-7

#### Story
The psalmist has waited so long and wept so much that his eyes are failing - worn out from watching for a God who has not yet come. Here waiting has a body: it strains the eyes dim and runs alongside weary, throat-parched crying. This is faith at its most exhausted, hope held on even as the strength to hold it drains away. It is the portrait of a person at the very end of his endurance who still keeps looking for God.

### H2: Psa 69:16-22

#### Story
The psalmist waited for someone - anyone - to show him pity, and no one came; he looked for comforters and found not one. Here the same word used elsewhere for hoping in God is aimed at other people, and it comes back empty. The result is crushing loneliness, the kind that strips away every human support. It is the picture of a person who has reached out for sympathy, found only silence, and is left utterly alone - with God as the only refuge left.

### H2: Psa 71:1-10

#### Story
Now old, the psalmist looks back over his whole life and says to God: you have been my hope since I was young. This is not a hope discovered late in a crisis but one that has run the length of his years, twinned with a trust he has carried from youth. Because it is so long-standing, it becomes the ground of his plea - do not cast me off now that I am old. It is a person leaning, in old age, on a lifetime of hoping in the same God.

### H2: Psa 71:13-24

#### Story
Surrounded by enemies who want to see him fall, the psalmist plants his flag: but I will keep on hoping, and praise you more and more. His hope is not static - it climbs, and the more he hopes the louder his praise grows. He is sure God will revive and comfort him again, and that confidence is what fuels the rising song. It is a defiant, growing hope that answers scorn not with despair but with ever-increasing worship.

### H2: Psa 78:4-6

#### Story
The whole reason for telling the next generation what God has done is spelled out here: so that the children will grow up to put their hope in God. This hope is a settled leaning of the heart, and it comes bundled with two companions - not forgetting God's works, and keeping his commands. The aim is to shape young lives whose confidence rests on God rather than on themselves. It is hope as something deliberately handed down, meant to take root in children not yet born.

### H2: Psa 106:10-16

#### Story
Here the psalm shows what happens when waiting collapses: the fathers in the wilderness did not wait for God's counsel - they soon forgot what he had done and rushed ahead of him. This is the exact opposite of hopeful waiting: an impatience that refuses to trust God's timing, tied hand-in-hand with forgetting his works. The psalm does not soften it. It is the dark mirror of every faithful wait in these psalms - a people who could not hold still for God, and paid for it.

### H2: Psa 119:42-63

#### Story
Right through this long psalm the writer keeps coming back to one thing: his hope is in God's word. Even when begging that the truth not be taken from his mouth, he holds fast to it - my hope is in your rules. Hope here is a steady habit of the heart, and what makes it distinctive is where it rests: not on vague expectation but on the concrete promises God has spoken, and he keeps steadying himself there. The same note sounds again and again across the psalm. It is a person who has tied his whole future to the reliability of what God has said.

### H2: Psa 119:66-82

#### Story
The psalmist pictures other believers catching sight of him and being glad - and the reason for their joy is that he has staked everything on God's word. His hope here is something he has already lived out, and it has become visible: those who fear God see it and rejoice. Hope in one person's heart turns into encouragement for others. It is the picture of a life whose trust in God's promises quietly gladdens everyone who watches it.

### H2: Psa 119:84-102

#### Story
Even in this psalm of devotion to God's word, the enemy appears: the wicked are lying in wait to destroy the writer. It is the same word used elsewhere for patiently hoping in God, here turned into a patient hope for his ruin. But the verse sets a striking answer beside the threat - while they lurk, he quietly ponders God's teachings. It is a portrait of menace met not by panic but by meditation: their dark waiting against his settled attention to God's word.

### H2: Psa 119:104-116

#### Story
Here hope shows its tender underside: the psalmist begs God to hold him up as he promised, so that he may live and not be shamed in the hope he has placed in him. He knows that to hope is to risk disappointment, and he pleads that his hope in God's word not be the thing that fails him. Hope here rests entirely on God's promise and asks that promise to bear its weight. It is a person aware that everything he is counting on depends on God keeping his word.

### H2: Psa 119:151-176

#### Story
Near the end of the long psalm the writer says plainly: I am waiting for your salvation, LORD - and in the same breath, and I keep your commandments. His hope for rescue does not make him passive; it runs alongside obedient living. Looking to God to save and doing what God asks are, for him, one life. It is the picture of a person who waits for deliverance not with folded hands but with a life still bent to God's will.

### H2: Psa 130:1-8

#### Story
In this psalm the writer waits for the LORD and, in the very same line, hopes in his word - and the two belong together. The waiting would be bare on its own; hoping in God's promise gives it something firm to hold onto. So his expectation is not left hanging in the air but rests on what God has said. It is a person reaching out toward God with his whole self, but reaching onto the solid ground of a promise.

### H2: Psa 131:1-3

#### Story
The little psalm of a quieted heart ends by inviting everyone in: Israel, hope in the LORD, now and for all time. Just before, the writer described his soul as calmed and settled, like a weaned child resting against its mother. Now he turns that stilled contentment into a call for the whole people to hope the same way. It is a quiet, unhurried hope stretched out over all time - the peace of one settled heart offered to a nation.

### H2: Psa 146:1-10

#### Story
The psalm pronounces a blessing: how happy is the person whose help and hope rest on the God of Jacob. It sets this against trusting human leaders, whose breath departs and whose plans die with them the same day. Real, lasting happiness, it says, belongs to the one who fastens hope on the God who made everything and keeps faith forever. Hope here is measured by its object - and the whole point is that hoping in the right God is itself a kind of blessedness.

### H2: Psa 147:9-14

#### Story
The psalm says something surprising about what pleases God: not the strength of a warhorse or the speed of a runner, but people who fear him and put their hope in his faithful love. Hope here is a leaning-forward of the heart onto God's steady, covenant love, and it stands side by side with reverent awe. Where awe looks up, hope leans in - and God takes delight in exactly this. It is the picture of an inner life that trusts God's love, and finds that this very trust is what gladdens him.


---

## File: psalms__humility-lowliness-contrition__narratives.md

# humility-lowliness-contrition — Psalms narratives

### H2: Psa 18:27-28

#### Story
In these lines the poet describes a settled lowliness of heart - not a mood but a way of being. The picture is simple and stark: God rescues people who carry themselves humbly, and he brings down those whose eyes are proud. The poet quietly places himself among the humble, the rescued ones. Nothing is said about where this lowliness comes from, how intense it is, or any organ of the body it sits in - it is just the whole person's standing before God. And there is no separate reward tacked on: the rescue itself is the whole point. Humble people are saved; proud people are humbled - two inner postures, two very different outcomes.

### H2: Psa 25:5-22

#### Story
Here the poet says God leads and teaches people who are humble. The point is quietly profound: it is the lowly, teachable heart that God can actually guide - a proud heart cannot be taught. Being humble here is not a passing mood but a settled way of being, and it shows itself as willingness to be led. The poem does not explain how a person becomes humble, how deep the humility runs, or promise any extra reward for it. The whole gift is simply this: the humble get to be guided by God.

### H2: Psa 34:1-22

#### Story
The poet makes a surprising claim: when a person's heart is shattered and their spirit crushed, that is precisely when God is closest. Brokenness here is a real inner condition, not just bad luck, and it is not something that shuts God out - it is what draws him near to rescue. The poem does not say what caused the heartbreak or how severe it is; that is left open. What matters is the nearness itself: the crushed person is not abandoned but found. The very place we would expect God to withdraw is the place he comes closest.

### H2: Psa 37:1-12

#### Story
The poet promises that gentle, unassertive people will end up secure and, more than that, deeply at peace and glad. Their lowliness does not lead to being trampled and losing everything; it leads to a quiet, abundant contentment they actually enjoy. This is set against the angry, scheming person a few lines earlier who plots and grinds his teeth - restless and bitter inside. So two inner worlds are compared: the calm delight of the humble and the churning agitation of the malicious. The poem does not explain how a person becomes meek or how strong the feeling is; it simply says the reward is peace, and that peace is its own joy.

### H2: Psa 40:1-17

#### Story
The poet ends by openly admitting his own poverty and need - and then adds one crucial word: 'but'. He is poor and needy, but the Lord keeps him in mind and comes to his aid. So this is weakness owned without despair; he holds his low estate and a calm confidence at the same time. The poem does not explain how he became poor or how bad it is; it simply rests in the fact that God remembers him. Lowliness here is not the end of hope - it is the very place where hope quietly settles.

### H2: Psa 45:1-15

#### Story
In this royal wedding song, gentleness appears in a surprising place - at the heart of a warrior-king's cause. As he rides out in splendour, he rides for truth, for gentleness, and for what is right, all together. Strikingly, the poem does not show this gentleness doing anything or acting on anyone; it is simply named as one of the noble things the king stands for, taking its meaning from the company it keeps. Almost nothing is said about how it works inside a person - no cause, no intensity, no result. That near-silence is the point: here humility is held up as an emblem of true kingship, gentleness woven into majesty rather than something we watch unfold.

### H2: Psa 51:1-19

#### Story
After his great failure, the poet discovers what God actually wants: not animal sacrifices but a heart that has been broken open. Contrition here is deep and located right in the heart - a person crushed and humbled from the inside. And the astonishing thing is that this shattered heart is exactly what God will not turn away; it is the one offering he receives. The brokenness is not aimed at anything or anyone - it is simply the true state of a person who has come undone before God. This is the family's most inward picture of lowliness: not a public posture but a heart crushed to dust, and welcomed there.

### H2: Psa 69:26-32

#### Story
When the poet is rescued, he says the humble will see it and be glad. Here the lowly are not a single suffering person but a whole watching community of people who seek God. Seeing God come through for one of their own lifts them - their hearts revive. The gladness is not aimed at anything; it simply wells up at the sight of deliverance. This shows humility as something shared and contagious: one person's rescue becomes hope for all the lowly who are watching.

### H2: Psa 70:1-5

#### Story
The poet ends with a raw, urgent cry: 'I am poor and needy - hurry to me, God! Don't delay!' His neediness here is not quiet resignation but pressing entreaty; it drives him to beg God to move fast. God is named as his only help and rescuer - there is nowhere else to turn. The need is bound up with his poverty, two words for the same emptiness. This is dependence stripped bare: so empty of resources that all he can do is plead for God to come quickly.

### H2: Psa 74:17-23

#### Story
In this national lament, the poet prays that God will rescue the poor and afflicted so they can praise his name instead of slinking away in shame. Here the lowly are pictured not just crying out but lifting their voices in worship once help comes. Their poverty is bound up with the whole downtrodden community, and the hoped-for end of it all is praise. The poem does not dwell on how they became poor or how deep it runs; it fixes on the goal - that rescue would turn their humiliation into thanksgiving. The lament reaches for worship restored.

### H2: Psa 76:7-12

#### Story
When God rises to judge the earth, the poet says, his aim is to save all the humble - the lowly and oppressed everywhere. Their humility is the very condition his rescue reaches for. And this happens in the same breath as the proud being brought down: God cuts off the spirit of arrogant rulers even as he saves the low. So one act of judgment does two things at once - it lifts the humble and topples the proud. The poem does not explain how these people became humble; it simply names them as the ones God's justice is coming to rescue.

### H2: Psa 81:11-12

#### Story
This is humility turned inside out. God recalls that his people would not bend to him - they refused to submit. It was a set disposition of the will, not a slip: they would not listen and they would not yield. The refusal was aimed squarely at God himself. And the result, spelled out next, is chilling: God simply hands them over to their own stubborn hearts, letting them follow their own counsel. Here we see the inner being's power to say no - a will that will not bow, and is left to the hardness it chose.

### H2: Psa 82:1-8

#### Story
In this courtroom scene among the powers of heaven, God demands justice for the utterly destitute - people stripped bare, with nothing. Their condition is one of sheer want, and the charge is that the very authorities meant to defend them have failed, siding with the wicked instead. The destitute stand beside the afflicted, and their empty hands are a silent accusation against corrupt power. The poem does not say how they fell so low; it simply insists that maintaining their right is exactly the duty being neglected. Here lowliness is at its rawest - and it becomes a demand for justice.

### H2: Psa 86:2-7

#### Story
David begins by telling God plainly: 'I am poor and needy' - and makes that his reason for asking God to listen. Here neediness is owned openly and laid right before God, not hidden. It sits paired with his poverty, two words for the same emptiness, and together they become his claim on God's attention. He is not ashamed of his lack; he presents it as the ground of his appeal. The very fact that he has nothing is why he throws himself entirely on God.

### H2: Psa 88:14-18

#### Story
This is the bleakest voice in the collection. The poet says he is helpless - undone, paralysed by terror, at the very end of himself. Unlike almost every other cry of the lowly, no rescue follows: there is no lifting, no nearness, no answer recorded here. His helplessness simply stands, wrapped in dread and lifelong suffering. He still cries out to God, but the light does not come in these lines. It is an honest picture of collapse - a person emptied of every resource, with no relief in sight.

### H2: Psa 89:16-24

#### Story
Here the word 'humble' flips to its harsh side: it is what the wicked try to do to God's chosen king - to beat him down and abase him. Their aim is oppression, driven by scheming enmity. But the poem immediately blocks it: the wicked will not succeed in humbling him, because God's covenant guards him. So the attempt is real but defeated; the intended humiliation never lands. This shows the darker face of the word - not a lowly heart to be cherished, but a blow aimed at another and turned aside.

### H2: Psa 90:5-17

#### Story
This poem finds humility in the plainest fact of all: human beings are dust, and God turns them back to it with a single word - 'Return.' Here lowliness is not about suffering or repentance but about what a person simply is - a fragile creature before an eternal God. Mankind as a whole is in view, and it is God himself who reduces us again to the ground. There is no drama of rescue here, just the quiet, sobering truth of our mortality. Beneath every other picture of the lowly lies this one: we are small, and we return to dust at God's command.

### H2: Psa 102:12-16

#### Story
The poet says that God pays attention to the prayer of the destitute - people stripped of everything - and does not brush their pleading aside. Here being destitute is a bare, emptied condition, yet it is precisely the condition God regards. Their poverty does not shut the door; it is bound up with a prayer that God actually honours. The poem does not explain how they came to have nothing; it simply marvels that God stoops to hear such people. Once again the lowest are the ones God turns toward.

### H2: Psa 106:38-48

#### Story
Recounting Israel's history, the poet says the ancestors were brought low - forced into submission under their enemies, crushed beneath foreign power. Here being humbled is not a chosen virtue but an imposed defeat, the bitter result of their repeated rebellion. Their subjection is bound up with real oppression at their enemies' hands. Yet the wider poem remembers that even this low point was not the end - God still turned back to them in mercy. It is a sobering picture: a whole people humbled by hard judgment.

### H2: Psa 107:37-43

#### Story
In this psalm's triumphant closing, the poet celebrates how God lifts the needy up out of their misery. Here their neediness is a low, afflicted state - and it is exactly what God reverses, raising them high and letting their families flourish like flocks. Their affliction is not the end of the story; it is the very thing God overturns. This is the bright opposite of despair: the poor lifted, the humble exalted. It is one of the collection's clearest pictures of God turning the lowest into the blessed.

### H2: Psa 109:14-22

#### Story
The poet accuses his enemy of a specific cruelty: he hunted down the poor, the needy, and the brokenhearted - chasing the very people least able to defend themselves. Here neediness is the mark of a helpless victim, someone the wicked man exploited rather than pitied. The need stands together with poverty and a crushed heart, a threefold portrait of the defenceless. The horror is that this weakness, which should have drawn mercy, instead drew persecution. It exposes the dark heart of the oppressor.

### H2: Psa 109:28-31

#### Story
The psalm ends on a note of rescue: God stands at the right hand of the needy one to defend and save him. Earlier an accuser had stood at that same right-hand place to condemn - now God takes it to protect. Here neediness is a low estate that God himself comes to champion, and his standing-by means the person's very life is saved. The one who was hunted for his poverty is now guarded by God. It is the turning of the whole poem: the defenceless finds a defender in God.

### H2: Psa 113:1-9

#### Story
This joyful psalm pictures God stooping down from heaven to lift the needy right out of the ash heap - the rubbish pile - and set them among princes. Here neediness is the lowest imaginable place, and it is exactly where God reaches to raise people up. The needy stand together with the poor lifted from the dust, a doubled image of God's great reversal. Nothing is said about how they sank so low; the wonder is entirely in the lifting. From the ash heap to a place of honour - this is God delighting to exalt the lowest.

### H2: Psa 132:4-14

#### Story
In this psalm about God choosing Zion as his home, he promises to satisfy her poor with bread. Here the poor are the needy people of God's own city, and his answer to their poverty is plain, abundant provision - they will be fed. Their lack is met not by rescue from enemies but by God simply filling them at the place he has made his dwelling. It is a quiet, homely picture of grace: the hungry poor given enough. Where God settles, the lowly are satisfied.

### H2: Psa 138:5-8

#### Story
The poet captures the paradox at the heart of it all: though God is exalted high above everything, it is the lowly he pays attention to. The humble, low-placed person is exactly the one the high God draws near to regard. And the opposite is just as true: the proud, for all their height, he keeps at a distance, knowing them only from afar. So rank is turned upside down - nearness to God goes not to the great but to the small. This one line sums up the whole pattern: lowliness pulls God close; pride pushes him away.

### H2: Psa 147:3-7

#### Story
This psalm of praise says God heals the brokenhearted and binds up their wounds - like a doctor tending an injury. Here a shattered heart is treated as a real inner wound, and God's response is to mend it, not just to sit nearby. The injury is genuine and named, and God actively repairs it. Where another poem pictured God simply drawing close to the crushed, this one shows him binding the wound and making it whole. Both are true: the broken heart both draws God near and gets healed by him.

### H2: Psa 149:1-6

#### Story
This final praise-psalm reaches the warmest note: God takes delight in the humble and adorns them with salvation, like dressing them in beauty. Here lowliness is not just rescued or lifted but treasured - God actually enjoys the humble and crowns them with his help. Their low posture becomes the very thing God delights to beautify. Nothing is said about how they became humble; the wonder is God's pleasure in them. Of all the pictures of the lowly, this is the tenderest: the humble, delighted in and dressed in rescue.


---

## File: psalms__inner-seat-heart-soul-spirit__narratives.md

# inner-seat-heart-soul-spirit — Psalms narratives

### H2: Psa 3:1-8

#### Story
In this psalm, 'many are saying of my SOUL, there is no SALVATION for him in God' - the sharpest attack is aimed at the interior: not the body but the soul's confidence is targeted. The soul is caught up in this: the enemies assault the soul's hope, saying there is no salvation for it in God - the interior under siege of despair-talk. Its reach is besieged-hope. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 6:1-10

#### Story
In this psalm, 'my SOUL also is greatly TROUBLED. But you, O LORD - how long?' - read distinct from the bones: the trouble has penetrated past the body to the soul, and the cry becomes 'how long?'. The soul is caught up in this: beyond the bones, the soul itself is greatly troubled - the disturbance reaching the innermost self. Its reach is inner-turmoil. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 7:1-17

#### Story
In this psalm, 'My shield is with God, who saves the UPRIGHT in HEART' - the movement locates safety not in strength but in a straight interior; the heart's uprightness is the qualification for rescue. The heart is caught up in this: the ground of confidence: God saves the upright in heart - the interior's uprightness is what it rests on. Its reach is uprightness. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 10:1-8

#### Story
In this psalm, 'He SAYS in his HEART, I shall not be MOVED; throughout all generations I shall not meet adversity' - the interior grants itself an untouchable stability, denial of any reckoning to come. The says is caught up in this: he says in his heart he will never be moved, never meet adversity - a self-assured permanence. Its reach is false-security. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 10:10-18

#### Story
In this psalm, 'He SAYS in his HEART, God has FORGOTTEN, he has hidden his face, he will never SEE it' - read distinct from v6's false security: this is specifically the belief that God does not observe or judge, the engine of the whole cruelty. The says is caught up in this: he says in his heart God has forgotten, has hidden his face, will never see it - the conviction of impunity. Its reach is impunity-conviction. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 12:1-4

#### Story
In this psalm, 'with FLATTERING lips and a DOUBLE HEART they speak' - the movement is a split interior: the smooth lips and the divided heart, saying one thing and being another. The double is caught up in this: everyone speaks lies to his neighbour with flattering lips and a heart-and-heart (double) - inner duplicity. Its reach is duplicity. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 16:1-11

#### Story
In this psalm, 'For you will not ABANDON my SOUL to Sheol, or let your holy one see corruption' - the interior's confidence extends beyond the grave; trust that death is not the end of the relation. The soul is caught up in this: the self is confident God will not abandon its soul to Sheol nor let his holy one see corruption - trust reaching past death. Its reach is death-defying-trust. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 19:7-14

#### Story
In this psalm, 'Let the words of my mouth and the MEDITATION of my HEART be ACCEPTABLE in your sight' - the movement offers the whole inner life (speech and thought) to God as an oblation seeking his approval. The heart is caught up in this: the self desires that the words of its mouth and the meditation of its heart be acceptable to God - the interior offered for approval. Its reach is self-offering. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 20:1-9

#### Story
In this psalm, 'May he grant you your HEART'S DESIRE and FULFIL all your plans' - the movement lifts the king's inmost want to God, asking that the deepest wish be met. The heart’s desire is caught up in this: the people ask God to grant the king his heart's desire and fulfil all his plans - a longing carried to God. Its reach is desire-granted. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 22:13-20

#### Story
In this psalm, 'my HEART is like wax; it is MELTED within my breast' - the movement is the loss of all inner firmness; the core of courage has run to liquid. The heart is caught up in this: the heart is like wax, melted within the breast - courage and resolve dissolving under terror. Its reach is dissolution. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 23:1-6

#### Story
In this psalm, 'He RESTORES my SOUL; he leads me in paths of righteousness' - the movement is the soul brought back, revived after depletion, the interior renewed by the shepherd's care. The soul is caught up in this: the shepherd restores/revives the self's soul, leading it in right paths - inner renewal received. Its reach is restoration. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 25:5-22

#### Story
In this psalm, 'His SOUL shall abide in WELL-BEING, and his offspring shall inherit the land' - the movement is rest: the fearing interior settles into a lasting good. Its reach is like grass. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

#### Story

What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

#### Story
In this psalm, 'you formed my INWARD PARTS (kidneys=core)' - the interior core is the thing being made; the self is authored before it can author. The inward parts is caught up in this: the inmost core of emotion/conscience is woven in the womb, the self prior to itself. Its reach is God's forming. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 139:21-24

#### Story
In this psalm, 'SEARCH me, O God, and know my HEART' - the movement reverses: having judged others, the self submits its own interior to examination. The heart is caught up in this: after the zeal, the self turns the searchlight inward, exposing its own heart. Its reach is self-exposure. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

### H2: Psa 141:4-10

#### Story
In this psalm, 'let not my HEART incline to any evil, to busy myself with wicked deeds' - the deeper guard: not the mouth only but the desire, refusing even to relish the delicacies of the wicked. The heart is caught up in this: the heart is fenced against being drawn toward evil deeds and the company of evildoers. Its reach is heart-guarding. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.

#### Story

The spirit is caught up in this: the spirit fails/faints inside while overwhelmed - the strength of the inner self ebbing. Its reach is spirit-failing. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 143:1-12

#### Story
In this psalm, 'For your name's sake, O LORD, preserve my life; bring my SOUL out of TROUBLE' - the interior stakes its rescue on God's honour, not its own worth. The soul is caught up in this: for God's name's sake the self asks to be preserved and its soul brought out of trouble. Its reach is deliverance-longing. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. It shows a dark turn of the inner life, and it is worth facing squarely rather than looking away.

### H2: Psa 146:1-10

#### Story
In this psalm, 'PRAISE the LORD, O my SOUL' - the movement is reflexive: the will rouses the soul, praise begun by self-address. The soul is caught up in this: the self commands its own soul to praise - the interior turned on itself as summons. Its reach is self-summons. The psalm does not tell us where inside a person this sits, how strong it was, or how it finally turned out. What it shows is something real happening deep inside a person.
