A few lines on, the same idea returns: the blessed do no wrong but walk in his ways. The passage does not measure the walk or trace where it begins; it simply holds up the happy life as one that moves, step by step, along the path God's word marks out.

### H2: Psa 119:20-25

#### Story
Psalm 119 turns to the proud and names their defining fault: the insolent, the accursed, are those who wander from God's commandments. Here wandering is the settled bent of arrogant people - not a stumble but a way of life that drifts away from God's word. And it is exactly what puts them under a curse. Just a few lines earlier the psalmist had prayed that he himself would not wander; now he shows us the people who actually do, and their straying is their ruin. The passage does not measure it or trace its origin; it simply holds up the proud as the living picture of the very danger the faithful dread - a life that has wandered off God's path and been cursed for it.

### H2: Psa 119:27-32

#### Story
In Psalm 119 the psalmist looks past mere plodding obedience to something faster and gladder: I will run in the way of your commandments - when you enlarge my heart. Here obedience is quickened from a walk into a run, eager movement along God's path. But he names the condition: this only happens when God frees and widens his heart. A cramped heart trudges; an enlarged one runs. The running and all his talk of walking and keeping belong to one picture, with running as its most joyful extreme. The passage does not measure the pace or file a separate cause beyond the freed heart; it simply shows a man longing to move swiftly and gladly in God's way, once God has made his heart large enough to do it.

### H2: Psa 119:42-63

#### Story
Deep in Psalm 119 the psalmist makes a striking claim: I shall walk in a wide place, because I have sought your precepts. Here walking is freedom - not the cramped, careful step of someone hemmed in, but movement through open, spacious country. And the surprise is what gives him the room: it is precisely because he has bound himself to God's word that his life opens out. Obedience, far from shrinking his world, widens it. The passage does not measure the freedom or file a separate cause beyond his seeking of God's precepts; it simply shows a man who has found that living by God's word is not a narrow confinement but a wide, open place to walk in.

### H2: Psa 119:66-82

#### Story
Psalm 119 contains a candid confession: before I was afflicted, I went astray. Here the psalmist looks back on his untroubled days and admits that, without hardship to steady him, he wandered off God's path. The striking thing is what set him right - not comfort but affliction. The suffering he would once have avoided is exactly what corrected his straying and brought him back to keep God's word. He does not blame anyone else; he owns the wandering as his own former state. The passage does not measure how far he strayed or explain how it started; it simply shows a man honest enough to say that ease let him drift and pain brought him home - the straying named as the condition his suffering cured.

### H2: Psa 119:104-116

#### Story
In Psalm 119 the psalmist reports both a danger and his answer to it: the wicked have set a trap for me, but I do not stray from your precepts. Here not-straying is a steadfastness held under pressure - a refusal to wander off God's path even when others are working to make him fall. The snare is real, the temptation to leave the path is real, and still he keeps his footing. This is the bright counter to all the straying the psalm elsewhere laments: where the proud drift and are cursed, this man stands firm precisely where he is most likely to slip. The passage does not measure his steadfastness or trace its source; it simply shows a person keeping to God's word under attack, unmoved by the trap laid to unsettle him.

### H2: Psa 119:118-123

#### Story
Psalm 119 records a hard word about those who wander: God spurns all who go astray from his statutes. Here straying is a settled turning-away from God's word, and the passage does not soften what it brings - God rejects those who do it. It is the dark counterpart to the psalmist's own longing to stay on the path: where he prays to keep God's word, these are the people who leave it, and their leaving meets God's refusal. The passage does not measure the straying or trace its cause; it simply states plainly that to wander from God's statutes is to be cast off by him - the wandering weighed here not as a small drift but as the thing God turns away from.

### H2: Psa 119:151-176

#### Story
For all its long devotion to God's law, Psalm 119 ends not on a note of triumph but of confession: I have gone astray like a lost sheep; seek your servant. Here the psalmist owns his own wandering - not the straying of the proud or the wicked, but his own, pictured as a sheep that has drifted from the flock. And he does not claim he can find his own way back; he asks God to come looking for him. It is a humble, honest close to a psalm full of high resolve, admitting that even the one who loves God's word can wander and needs to be sought. The passage does not measure the straying or explain it; it simply shows a man confessing he is lost and trusting God to seek him out.

### H2: Psa 125:3-5

#### Story
Psalm 125 names a quiet danger. If wicked rule presses down on God's people too long, the psalm says, even the righteous might stretch out their hands to do wrong. Here the reaching is a temptation, not yet a deed - the point at which drawn-out oppression could finally bend an upright person toward wrongdoing. It lives as an inner pressure on people who mean to stay good but are being worn down. That is exactly why the psalm trusts God not to let the wicked's rule rest on the land too long: to spare the righteous from being pushed past their breaking point. The passage does not measure the danger or say it actually happened; it simply shows the honest possibility that prolonged injustice can tempt even good people to reach for wrong, and God's mercy in cutting it short.

### H2: Psa 128:1-2

#### Story
Psalm 128 opens with a blessing on everyone who fears the Lord and walks in his ways. Here walking is the fear of God made visible - reverence that does not stay locked inside but shows itself in a whole manner of life along God's paths. Fearing God and walking in his ways are not two things but one: the awe produces the walk, and the walk is what the awe looks like from the outside. This is the blessed life. The passage does not measure the walk or file a separate source beyond the fear itself; it simply shows that true reverence for God naturally becomes a way of living, and pronounces happy the person in whom the two are joined.

### H2: Psa 131:1-3

#### Story
Psalm 131 gives a picture of a quieted, humble heart, and one line shows what that humility does: I do not busy myself with things too great and too marvellous for me. Here the idea of walking-among becomes a matter of not meddling - the psalmist refuses to grasp at matters beyond his measure, to reach for what is over his head. It is ambition deliberately set aside, a settled contentment to stay within his own limits. This refusal lives in the inner life as a kind of restful humility. The passage does not measure it or explain where it came from; it simply shows a person who has stopped straining after greatness and marvels too big for him, choosing instead to keep quietly to what is his - the walk narrowed, on purpose, by humility.

### H2: Psa 138:5-8

#### Story
Psalm 138 makes a realistic promise. The psalmist does not say God keeps him out of trouble; he says, though I walk in the midst of trouble, you preserve my life. Here walking is a going straight through danger rather than around it - the path leads into the trouble, and he keeps moving through the heart of it. And bound to that walk is God's preserving: the going-through and the being-kept-alive are one and the same movement. He is not spared the danger; he is carried through it. The passage does not measure the trouble or trace where the walk began; it simply shows a man who expects to pass right through hard and threatening places, and who trusts that God will keep him alive as he goes - preservation not from the trouble but in the very midst of it.

### H2: Psa 139:4-6

#### Story
Psalm 139 turns over a startling thought: where could I go to get away from God? Here fleeing is an inner experiment of the will - the psalmist runs the idea out in every direction, testing whether there is anywhere he could escape God's presence. He tries the heights and the depths, the far side of the sea, the dark - and everywhere he looks, God is already there. The impulse to flee is real, but it is inseparable from its own failure: every route he imagines turns out to have no exit. The passage does not measure the urge or say what prompted it; it simply shows a mind testing the possibility of escaping God and discovering, by the very attempt, that there is nowhere God is not - the wish to flee resolving into the recognition that flight is impossible.

## File: psalms__wickedness-ungodliness__narratives.md

# wickedness-ungodliness — Psalms narratives

### H2: Psa 7:1-17

#### Story
The psalm pictures the inner life of a wicked man as a kind of pregnancy. He conceives evil the way a mother conceives a child - it starts as something carried inside, in the mind, long before it is seen. Then he is pregnant with mischief, and at last he gives birth to lies. The harm is not something done to him or planted in him by anyone else; the passage never says where it comes from - it simply grows out of the man himself. It is his whole self that does this, not just one part of him. And once evil is conceived, it will be born: carrying it and delivering it are one unbroken thing. There is no measuring of how much or how fierce, and no note here of where it ends up, though the verses that follow show his mischief falling back on his own head. What the passage fixes on is the generation itself - a settled bent of the inner life that keeps producing evil from within, as naturally and inevitably as a body brings a child to term.

### H2: Psa 37:14-40

#### Story
Here evil appears from a different angle than usual - not as something a wicked person is busy doing, but as the very thing a person is told to walk away from. The command is simple and double: turn away from evil, and do good. It is an act of the will, a decision to change direction. And the two halves belong together - you cannot really leave evil behind without at the same time moving toward good; they are one turn, not two. The passage does not say where the evil came from or how hard the turning is; it just issues the charge and holds out its promise, that the one who turns will dwell secure for good. What it fixes on is the reorientation itself, a settled inward direction the person is called to take and keep.

### H2: Psa 40:1-17

#### Story
This is evil felt from the inside as being drowned. The man is not doing wrong here - he is buried under it. Troubles have surrounded him past counting, and his own sins have caught up with him until he cannot even see; there are more of them, he says, than the hairs on his head, and his heart gives out. The passage does not sort out where it all comes from or separate his own guilt from the troubles pressing in; it simply reports the flood. The sheer, unmeasured weight of it is the point - too many to number, enough to make his courage fail. And the guilt and the drowning are one thing: it is exactly the mass of his iniquity that swamps him. This is evil as an overwhelming condition of the inner life, a man going under.

### H2: Psa 45:1-15

#### Story
This is wickedness seen from the opposite side - not as something a bad man is doing, but as the thing a good king cannot stand. The line says he has loved righteousness and hated wickedness, and that hatred is the real subject here. Wickedness itself does nothing in this scene: it has no plan, no victim, no force of its own. It shows up only as the object the king's heart rejects, the exact opposite of the goodness he loves. The two are set against each other like poles, and what the passage actually shows us is the inner recoil of a righteous person - the settled turning-away that is the flip side of loving what is right. Wickedness is present only as the thing being refused.

### H2: Psa 50:13-23

#### Story
God turns and speaks directly to a certain kind of person: the wicked one who keeps reciting God's laws and taking his covenant on his lips - and God asks what right he has to do it. The passage does not point to one particular crime here. It condemns the man for what he is: someone who performs religion while his life runs the other way, who hates correction and throws God's words behind him even as he mouths them. He is not caught doing a single deed; he is exposed as a whole - a hypocrite, religion without obedience. And he stands here as an outsider, another person God addresses across the room, not an inner life described from within. The silence about any specific act is the point: it is his condition, not his conduct in this moment, that is on trial.

### H2: Psa 51:1-19

#### Story
This is the rare place where evil is not somebody else's - it is the speaker's own, confessed. He says plainly that he has done what is evil in God's sight. What makes it evil is not what people think of it, but that God saw it: the deed is measured against God's own eyes. And he binds this admission to another - that his sin is, in the end, against God alone. The two belong together: the wrong he did and the fact that it was done before God are one thing. He does not here explain how he came to it; he simply owns it. This is evil turned inward and upward at once - a man judging himself by God's standard and naming his own act for what it is.

### H2: Psa 52:1-9

#### Story
This is evil worn as a boast. The psalm confronts a powerful man who is proud of his own wickedness - he brags about it. Evil, for him, is not something to hide but something to show off, a badge of his strength. The passage ties this boasting to what it says a moment later: that he actually loves evil more than good. The two belong together - to boast in evil and to love it are one settled bent of his heart. There is no victim named in this particular line and no explaining where it came from; the point is simply that his wickedness has become his pride. This is the inner life of a man so far gone that his evil is his glory.

### H2: Psa 53:1-6

#### Story
The passage opens with the fool who says in his heart there is no God, and then shows what that denial looks like in practice: they are corrupt, and they do abominable things. The word for abominable means loathsome, sickening to God - not just wrong but repulsive. And it is a doing, a habit, not a passing thought: they practise iniquity. The loathsome deeds and the inner corruption belong together; the vile behaviour is simply the outward face of a heart that has decided God is not there. Strip away the belief in God, the passage says, and this is what comes out - conduct God finds detestable.

### H2: Psa 54:1-7

#### Story
The psalmist is confident that the harm his enemies planned will come straight back on them. Their evil is real - it is their own settled malice, the harm they devised against him. But he trusts God to return it to them, to turn it back on their own heads. What is striking is that the evil's next move is put in God's hands: it is God who will send it home. There is no new victim in view; the enemies simply receive back what they intended for another. This is poetic justice - the wickedness of the wicked recoiling on the ones who made it, and God the one who bends it back.

### H2: Psa 55:1-5

#### Story
The prayer begins in distress, and the cause is named at once: the noise of the enemy and the oppression of the wicked. These are godless men bearing down on the psalmist - their clamour and the trouble they keep dropping on him are one thing, a steady hostile pressure. It is aimed straight at him. The passage does not describe their inner world so much as their weight on his: they stand outside him as the force pressing in, and the next lines show what it does - his heart writhes, terror and trembling fall on him. Here the wicked are simply the ones leaning their whole hostility onto a man until he is overwhelmed.

### H2: Psa 55:9-23

#### Story
This line does not show the enemies doing evil so much as show where evil lives: in their homes and in their hearts. It has moved in and made itself at home among them. The passage is not reporting an action here - it is reporting a residence, a settled presence. Evil is simply there, lodged at their core, filling the place where they live. And that is exactly why the psalmist calls down such a harsh judgment on them, that death would suddenly overtake them: because the wickedness is not a passing act they might repent of but a permanent tenant of their inner life. The most telling thing is what is not said - evil here is not doing anything; it is simply dwelling in them.

### H2: Psa 56:1-13

#### Story
The psalmist says of his enemies that all their thoughts are against him for evil. It is not one scheme but the whole bent of their minds: everything they think tends toward doing him harm. Evil here is the settled aim behind their thinking, malice that occupies their inner life and points, always, at him. The passage ties this to what it calls their crime a couple of lines later - the guilt their malice earns, which God's wrath will answer. So the evil is both the direction of their thoughts and the thing that makes them guilty. What is caught here is a mind wholly given over to plotting harm against another person.

### H2: Psa 58:1-5

#### Story
This psalm looks at wicked people and traces their corruption all the way back to birth. They are, it says, estranged from the womb - they go astray from the day they are born, speaking lies as soon as they can. The point is that their wickedness is not something they picked up along the way; it is built in, present from the start. The passage does not describe one particular crime here - it describes a nature, a settled condition that shows itself early in straying and lying. And it looks ahead to how such wickedness is stopped: not by reform but by God breaking their fangs, the way you would draw the teeth of a venomous snake. This is the wicked read at their root, corrupt from the beginning.

### H2: Psa 59:1-5

#### Story
Saul's men are watching the psalmist's house to kill him, and he prays to be delivered from those who work evil. Evil here is their occupation - iniquity is what they do, their trade. And the passage sets it next to bloodthirst: the working of wickedness and the appetite for killing are one company, inseparable. He does not spell out how they came to this; he simply names them as workers of iniquity and murderous men, and asks God to save him from them. What is caught is a settled condition of the inner life that expresses itself as a livelihood of harm, sharpened here by the thirst for blood.

### H2: Psa 60:9-12

#### Story
This appears in the wickedness-family because it is salvation read in the negative - a hope exposed as empty. The people, feeling that God has rejected them and no longer marches with their armies, come to a hard, honest conclusion: human rescue is worthless. Whatever help men can give is, they say, vanity. It is a judgment they reach in their own minds, a disillusioned but true estimate. And it is set as the exact opposite of the next line, where with God they will do valiantly. The collapse of human hope and the turn to God are two sides of one coin. What is caught here is the moment a community stops trusting in human deliverance and, by stripping that false hope away, clears the ground for confidence in God alone.

### H2: Psa 64:1-6

#### Story
The psalm looks into the inner world of plotters who whet their tongues like swords and shoot their words from ambush, and at the centre of it is their resolve: they hold fast to their evil plan. The evil here is the fixed aim they cling to - the settled purpose behind all their scheming and their hunt for a way to do harm. The resolve and the wicked plan cannot be pulled apart: the evil exists here precisely as the thing they will not let go of. They dig in, brace themselves, encourage each other in it. What is caught is a determined inner commitment to wrongdoing, evil held onto as a settled purpose.

### H2: Psa 68:1-7

#### Story
The psalm opens with God rising and his enemies scattering, and the wicked are pictured at the receiving end of it: like wax melting before a fire, they perish before God. The striking thing is that it takes no struggle - his mere presence undoes them, the way heat melts wax. They are lumped together with those who hate God; the wicked and the God-haters are one crowd, and both simply dissolve when he appears. Set against them are the righteous, who are glad and exult. What is caught here is the wicked at their end - not doing anything, but coming undone before a God whose nearness they cannot survive.

### H2: Psa 71:1-10

#### Story
An old man, his strength spent, prays to be rescued from the hand of the wicked - from the grasp of a cruel and unjust man. Here the wicked are not shown doing a particular thing; they are a menace, a grip closing on him, and he pleads to be pulled free of it. What marks them is their character: unjust and cruel. And they are the same enemies who, a few lines later, are watching for his life and saying God has abandoned him. What is caught is the wicked as a looming threat from outside - a hostile presence pressing on a vulnerable man who has nowhere to turn but God.

### H2: Psa 73:1-8

#### Story
The psalmist almost lost his footing over this: he looked at wicked people and saw them prospering. They have no pain, their bodies are healthy and sleek, they are not in trouble like everyone else. Their ease is the thing that caught him - it seemed to make faith pointless. And that prosperity does not sit quietly; it breeds arrogance. Pride becomes their necklace, violence their clothing, and they scoff and threaten. The comfort and the contempt go together. What is caught here is the wicked not in a particular crime but in their untroubled flourishing - the scandal of the godless doing well, which nearly overturned the psalmist's faith until he went into God's presence and saw their end.

### H2: Psa 75:4-10

#### Story
God himself speaks to the wicked with a warning: do not lift up your horn - do not raise your strength in arrogance, do not speak with a proud, stiff neck. The horn is a picture of self-exalting power, and the wicked are told to stop flaunting it. The warning is bound to what comes later: it is God who lifts one up and puts another down, and in the end he will cut off the horns of the wicked. So the arrogant strength they are warned about is the very thing the Judge will one day break. What is caught here is the wicked at the moment of warning - their proud self-assertion named and forbidden by the God who has the power to humble them.

### H2: Psa 82:1-8

#### Story
God stands in the divine council and charges the judges with a corruption: how long will you judge unjustly and show partiality to the wicked? Here the wicked are seen from an unusual angle - not as the ones committing a crime, but as the ones being protected, the guilty whom a crooked court lets off. Their very identity in this line is the injustice done for them: they are the favoured evildoer, shielded instead of sentenced. The wickedness and the partiality that covers it are bound together. What is caught is the inner reality of the guilty who benefit from corruption - the wicked man whose escape from justice is itself the wrong being exposed.

### H2: Psa 88:1-5

#### Story
This is one of the darkest of the psalms, and it opens with a man saying his soul is full of troubles and his life is nearly at the grave. The word here is the same one used elsewhere for evil, but there is no wrongdoing in view - only suffering. His inner self is crowded, filled to the brim with troubles, so packed with them that death feels close. The passage does not say where they came from; it just reports the fullness. And the troubles and the soul that holds them cannot be separated - his very condition is a self overrun with them. What is caught here is not wickedness done but evil endured, a person weighed down with trouble until he stands at the edge of the pit.

### H2: Psa 88:7-12

#### Story
The word here usually names something detestable, but in this psalm it lands on the sufferer himself: God, he says, has made him a horror to his friends. They shun him; he has become repugnant to the very people who should be closest. It is not that he has done something loathsome - it is that his affliction has turned him into an object others recoil from. And it comes bound to another misery: he is shut in, unable to escape. The revulsion of his friends and his own trapped isolation go together. What is caught here is abandonment at its sharpest - a man so afflicted that those who loved him now turn away, and he is left detestable and alone.

### H2: Psa 92:1-12

#### Story
The stupid and the foolish cannot see it, the psalm says, but here is the truth: though the wicked spring up as fast and thick as grass and every evildoer flourishes, they are only racing toward destruction. The quick growth is not a sign of strength but of shallowness - grass that shoots up and is gone. Their flourishing and their ruin are the same story. What looks like the wicked doing well is really the wicked hastening their own end. The passage sets their bloom next to the brief flourishing of all evildoers, and reads both the same way: a fast, showy rise that runs straight to being cut down forever.

### H2: Psa 94:2-5

#### Story
The psalm cries out for God to rise up as judge, and the reason is the wicked's unchecked triumph: how long, it asks twice over, will the wicked exult? They are gloating, celebrating in their power, and their triumph is bound up with their oppression - they pour out arrogant words and crush God's people. What galls is not just that they do harm but that they get away with it and revel in it, on and on. The exulting is the very face of their inner condition. What is caught here is the wicked at the peak of their impunity, so long unpunished that the sufferer can only ask how long it will be allowed to last.

### H2: Psa 94:7-23

#### Story
This line uses a different word for wicked, one that carries the sense of ruin, and it lands on people in power: can corrupt rulers be allied with you, God? These are authorities who do not merely break the law but write it - they frame injustice by statute, turning evil into legislation. And they do it as if God were on their side, as though their unjust laws carried his approval. That is the outrage the question exposes: wickedness sitting on a throne, dressing its injustice in the language of law and claiming divine sanction. What is caught here is evil that has captured authority - not a lone criminal but a corrupt power that makes wrongdoing official.

### H2: Psa 101:1-8

#### Story
The king who has pledged to walk with integrity ends his psalm with a hard vow: morning by morning he will destroy all the wicked in the land and cut off every evildoer from the city of the LORD. The wicked here are seen at their end - the ones to be rooted out. And the vow is relentless, a daily task: keeping God's city clean of evil is not a one-time act but a standing commitment renewed every morning. The wicked and the evildoers are named together, both marked for removal. What is caught is the wicked as the object of a righteous ruler's determination to purge them, so that no evil is allowed to settle in God's place.

### H2: Psa 104:31-35

#### Story
A long hymn celebrating the beauty and order of creation ends with one jarring wish: let sinners be consumed from the earth, and let the wicked be no more. After all the praise, the psalmist sees the wicked as the single thing spoiling the picture - the one discordant note in a world made to bless God. He does not describe a crime here; he simply prays that they cease to exist. Their being wicked at all is the problem. And they are named alongside the sinners, both wished away. What is caught is the wicked seen against the backdrop of a praising creation, longed to be gone so that everything that has breath may praise the LORD without a stain.

### H2: Psa 106:1-8

#### Story
This psalm turns the accusation inward. Instead of pointing at the wicked out there, the people say of themselves: both we and our fathers have sinned, we have committed iniquity, we have done wickedness. The same word used elsewhere for the ungodly is here owned in the first person, a full confession of guilt stretching back through the generations. And the wrong is directed at God - it is against him that the wickedness was done. The three admissions pile up together: sinned, committed iniquity, done wickedness. What is caught here is not somebody else's evil but the community's own, honestly named as the active wrong it was, wrongdoing confessed rather than blamed on another.

### H2: Psa 107:28-32

#### Story
The psalm surveys how God deals with the world, and among his acts is this: he turns a fruitful land into a salty waste because of the evil of the people who live there. Evil here is a settled disposition of a whole population - not one crime but a bent that marks them - and it provokes God to judge. The result is written on the very ground: rich country reduced to barren salt. Their inner wickedness and the ruined land are tied together; the waste is what their evil produces. What is caught here is evil as a communal condition heavy enough to unmake the land itself, the wickedness of the inhabitants drawing down a judgment that leaves the earth sterile.

### H2: Psa 107:37-43

#### Story
As the psalm celebrates how God lifts up the needy and pours contempt on princes, it pictures the response of wickedness in a single vivid image: it shuts its mouth. The upright see what God does and rejoice, but wickedness has nothing left to say - it is struck dumb. Here the wicked are personified as a mouth that is finally stopped, silenced by the sheer force of God's justice on behalf of the poor. There is no argument left, no boast, no accusation. What is caught is the moment of the wicked's speechless defeat, when God's setting-right of the world leaves evil with no reply.

### H2: Psa 109:1-8

#### Story
The psalm cries out because wicked and deceitful mouths have opened against the psalmist, attacking him with lying tongues and surrounding him with words of hate. Here the wickedness of his enemies comes out in speech - false accusation aimed straight at him, for no cause. They repay his love with hatred and his good with evil. The wickedness and the lying go together; it is through deceitful mouths that their inner evil reaches him. What is caught is the wicked as slanderers, their hostility taking the form of words - and against it the psalmist sets not counter-attack but prayer, giving himself to God while their mouths keep accusing.

### H2: Psa 109:14-22

#### Story
As the psalm prays for its enemies to be repaid, it names them once more: those who speak evil against my life. Evil here is malice carried in speech, and it is aimed at the highest stake - his life itself. Their words are not idle insults but an assault meant to destroy him. The evil and the accusers who utter it are named as one; the malice belongs to them. This is the ground on which the psalmist asks God to make all their curses recoil - let this evil speech be the very reward the LORD hands back to them. What is caught is the accusers' settled hostility expressed in words that seek a man's undoing.

### H2: Psa 112:1-10

#### Story
The whole psalm has praised the blessed, righteous man - secure, generous, unafraid - and it ends by turning to his opposite: the wicked man sees all this and is enraged. He watches the good man's blessing, and instead of being drawn to it he is galled by it; he gnashes his teeth and melts away. His anger is the anger of one who cannot bear another's good and has no share in it. And that very rage is bound up with his undoing - he melts, and his desire comes to nothing. What is caught here is the wicked as the envious foil to the blessed, consumed from within by fury at a goodness he will never possess.

### H2: Psa 119:42-63

#### Story
Across this long psalm the wicked keep reappearing as the antagonist of the man who loves God's law, and this line gathers them up: hot indignation seizes him because of the wicked, who forsake God's law. Two things go together in them - they abandon the law themselves, and they lay traps for him. Their defection from God and their hostility to the psalmist are one and the same movement. And they recur throughout the psalm: their cords try to ensnare him, they lie in ambush to destroy him, they set snares in his path, yet he does not forget or stray from the law. What is caught is the wicked as the standing foe of the faithful - law-forsakers whose abandonment of God burns the psalmist with indignation while their snares fail to move him.

### H2: Psa 119:84-102

#### Story
In a psalm full of the wicked and their snares, this line gives the answer from the other side: I hold back my feet from every evil way, in order to keep your word. Here the movement is not toward evil but away from it, and it is deliberate - the psalmist actively restrains himself, pulling his feet back from every path that would lead him wrong. And the reason is love for God's word: he refuses evil precisely so that he can keep it. This restraint is part of the whole shape of his devotion across the psalm. Against all the wickedness the psalm describes, this is the inner being's counter-move - a man curbing himself from evil for the sake of obeying God.

### H2: Psa 119:104-116

#### Story
The psalmist, devoted to God's law, draws a line against a certain company: depart from me, you evildoers, that I may keep the commandments of my God. The evildoers are named by what they do - evil is their settled practice - and it presses on him closely enough that he must send them away. He treats their company as a threat to his obedience: to keep God's commands, he has to be rid of them. So he pushes them outside, out of his life. What is caught here is evil-doing as the mark of a company the faithful must separate from - not analysed from within, but held at arm's length, banished so that a man can hold on to his God.

### H2: Psa 125:3-5

#### Story
This song of ascents pictures wickedness as a ruler's scepter stretched over the land given to God's people. It is oppressive power - wickedness in the seat of authority, pressing down on the righteous. But the promise is that it will not rest there for good: God will not let the rule of the wicked settle permanently over his people's land. And the reason given is tender and shrewd - lest the righteous themselves, worn down under long oppression, be tempted to reach out their own hands to do wrong. So God caps the tenure of wicked rule not only to spare his people harm but to guard their integrity. What is caught is wickedness as dominion, and the mercy of the limit God sets on it.

### H2: Psa 129:1-5

#### Story
Israel remembers a long history of affliction - greatly have they oppressed me from my youth, plowers plowing long furrows on my back - and then names the deliverance: the LORD is righteous; he has cut the cords of the wicked. Here the wicked are seen through their instrument, the cords with which they held the people bound, their oppression pictured as ropes. And the turning point is God's act: being righteous, he cuts those cords and undoes the binding. The wicked's grip and its severing are the whole story. What is caught is the wicked as oppressors whose hold on God's people is real but not final - ropes that a righteous God simply cuts through, setting the bound free.

### H2: Psa 139:12-19

#### Story
Psalm 139 has spent itself in wonder - God knowing the psalmist completely, forming him in the womb, thoughts toward him past counting - and then it turns sharply: O that you would slay the wicked, O God; depart from me, you men of blood. The wicked here are the bloodthirsty, and what the psalmist recoils from is not one crime but their whole bent - a settled inner orientation toward killing that defines them as men of blood. Their disposition and their thirst for blood are one thing. Having just contemplated the God who gives and knows life, he cannot abide those whose inmost bearing is set toward taking it. What is caught is the wicked read at the level of disposition, a murderous heart the psalmist wants nothing to do with.
